765: ========================================================================
766: PITFALL 48: Idle menu_input (key 0) MUST NOT unconditional bump_screen /
767:             hit_frame — causes multi-frame-per-second spam (unlike
768:             mutaclsym, which only composes when input/state changed)
769: ========================================================================
770: 
771: SYMPTOM: Terminal floods with `--- FRAME UPDATE at ... ---` markers even
772: when the user is idle. `pieces/display/frame_history.txt` grows to
773: megabytes in minutes. CPU sits high; scrollback becomes unusable.
774: 
775: ROOT CAUSE: House pal loops (login, START_BUTTON, forum-family) look like:
776: 
777:   loop:
778:     menu_input 0          # idle catch-up every ~30ms
779:     read_history ...
780:     ...
781:     compose_frame
782:     hit_frame
783: 
784: If `menu_input` key==0 always grows `*_screen_changed.txt` (or always
785: calls compose), the main loop takes the "render" branch on every sleep
786: tick — not only when something changed. mutaclsym avoids this: its
787: dispatch only composes after real keys / turn work.
788: 
789: LIVE EXAMPLE (user-pal login, 2026-07-27):
790:   userpal_menu_input key 0 did regenerate_login_pdl() then
791:   bump_screen() unconditionally → ~33 recomposes/sec with no new
792:   accounts and no keypresses.
793: 
794: FIX:
795:   1. Idle path may recompute catalogs, but pulse/recompose ONLY when
796:      the resulting view/catalog fingerprint actually differs
797:      (hash piece.pdl, compare last_message, compare layout, etc.).
798:   2. Real actions (KEY:n, RUN:, login success, href) still bump.
799:   3. Prefer mutaclsym shape when possible: compose only after input
800:      processing that changed state.
801: 
802: AUDIT: system/renderer.c appends every painted frame to
803:   pieces/display/frame_history.txt (cleared at renderer process start /
804:   each new session). Use that file to prove spam vs calm paint rates.
805:   START_BUTTON also dual-writes install debug/frame_history.txt via
806:   PRISC_FRAME_HISTORY (cleared each `./button.sh run`).
807: 
808: FILES: 0.user-pal/00.login-signup/ops/userpal_menu_input.c,
809:        *.START_BUTTON/ops/start_menu_input.c (idle already gated),
810:        pal/main_loop_chtpm.pal family, system/renderer.c
811: 
812: SEE ALSO: xyzos-standards sec. 20 (ONE VISIBLE FRAME WRITER), sec. below
813: on frame_history audit logs.

843: =======================================================================
844: PITFALL 50: prisc+x custom-op dispatch (exec_custom_op's popen()/
845:             system() calls) interpolates the project path UNQUOTED —
846:             any project living under a directory name containing a
847:             shell metacharacter silently loses EVERY custom op,
848:             forever, with zero error visible to the caller
849: =======================================================================
850: DATE: 2026-07-29
851: SEVERITY: CRITICAL (every op, every project, under the affected path —
852:            not a bug in the project's own code at all)
853: PROJECT FOUND: &.widgits/file-menu (this whole tree — event-editor,
854:            map-picker, tile-picker, proc-monitor — lives one directory
855:            deeper than every other project, inside a folder literally
856:            named "&.widgits" per PITFALL 49's own house convention)
857: 
858: SYMPTOM: file-menu's nav pickers rendered fine ([>]/[ ] showed cursor
859: position), arrow keys were correctly defined, but cursor_pos in
860: fm_state.txt NEVER moved no matter what was injected. The marker file
861: (pieces/display/fm_screen_changed.txt) that fm_menu_input.c touches on
862: every call stayed at 0 bytes forever — looked exactly like "the PAL
863: module never runs" or "read_history is broken." Spent a long time
864: chasing that hypothesis (checking <module> tag parsing, read_history's
865: literal-path form, the main_loop_chtpm.pal control flow byte-for-byte
866: against working siblings) before finding the real cause.
867: 
868: ROOT CAUSE (system/prisc+x.c, exec_custom_op()): every `void ops/+x/*`
869: custom op (fm_menu_input, fm_compose_frame, chain_menu_input, etc.) is
870: dispatched by building a shell command string with asprintf() and
871: running it through popen()/system():
872: 
873:   char *full_script_path; // = PRISC_PROJECT_ROOT + "/" + handler path
874:   asprintf(&cmd, "%s %d", full_script_path, regs[i->rs1]);
875:   FILE *pipe = popen(cmd, "r");   // full_script_path is NEVER quoted
876: 
877: PRISC_PROJECT_ROOT for file-menu resolves to a path containing a bare,
878: unquoted "&" (".../44.xyz.../&.widgits/file-menu/..."). /bin/sh treats
879: an unquoted "&" as "background the previous command, then run the
880: rest" — it silently SPLITS the exec path in two:
881: 
882:   sh: 1: .widgits/file-menu/.../fm_menu_input.+x: not found
883:   sh: 1: /home/no/Desktop/.../44.xyz.../: Permission denied
884: 
885: Confirmed by manually reproducing the exact popen() string with sh -c
886: outside prisc+x entirely — same two errors, byte for byte. Neither
887: error is printed anywhere the caller sees by default: chtpm_parser_pal
888: forks prisc+x with stdout redirected to /dev/null in every button.sh
889: (`>/dev/null 2>&1 &`), so the "not found"/"Permission denied" lines
890: vanish silently. Every custom op call becomes a complete, silent no-op:
891: the op's own binary never runs, so it never touches ITS OWN marker
892: file either — from the PAL loop's side, this is indistinguishable from
893: "the marker-diff gate is broken" or "the module isn't executing at
894: all," which is exactly the wrong trail this was chased down first.
895: 
896: WHY THIS AFFECTS THE WHOLE &.widgits/ FAMILY, NOT JUST file-menu: any
897: project under a path containing a shell metachar (&, ;, |, $, `, (),
898: unescaped spaces already handled by real dirs elsewhere in this tree
899: via quoting THAT never existed here) hits the identical silent failure
900: for EVERY custom op it defines, unconditionally — this is a property
901: of the path, not of any individual project's own PAL script or op
902: code. event-editor, map-picker, tile-picker, proc-monitor all sit at
903: the exact same "&.widgits/<name>" depth and are exposed identically
904: until the shared prisc+x binary is rebuilt from the fixed source.
905: 
906: FIX: quote the resolved path before it ever reaches popen()/system().
907: Added a small shell_quote() helper (single-quote wrap, with the
908: standard '\'' escape for any literal single quotes already present)
909: in system/prisc+x.c, applied to full_script_path at all three
910: cmd-building call sites inside exec_custom_op() (the layout_op branch,
911: the literal-arg branch, and the register-value branch):
912: 
913:   static char *shell_quote(const char *in) {
914:       size_t len = strlen(in);
915:       char *out = malloc(len * 4 + 3);
916:       char *o = out; *o++ = '\'';
917:       for (size_t k = 0; k < len; k++) {
918:           if (in[k] == '\'') { *o++='\''; *o++='\\'; *o++='\''; *o++='\''; }
919:           else *o++ = in[k];
920:       }
921:       *o++ = '\''; *o = '\0';
922:       return out;
923:   }
924:   ...
925:   char *quoted_script_path = shell_quote(full_script_path);
926:   asprintf(&cmd, "%s %d", quoted_script_path, regs[i->rs1]);
927: 
928: No-op for every path that never had a metacharacter in it (every
929: non-widget project) — this is purely additive safety, not a behavior
930: change for anything that currently works.
931: 
932: VERIFIED LIVE: after rebuilding system/prisc+x from the patched source
933: and pulling it into file-menu via its own build.sh (which copies the
934: compiled binary from 014.wsr-pal, the canonical shared-ops source),
935: fm_screen_changed.txt started growing every idle tick as expected, and
936: a real ARROW_DOWN injected through the standard pipeline (see PITFALL
937: 51 below for why the FIRST injection attempt looked broken too) moved
938: cursor_pos 1 -> 2 -> 5 across repeated key presses, matching every
939: other working project's own nav behavior exactly.
940: 
941: STANDING RULE: system/prisc+x.c is this family's own shared VM (see
942: that file's own top-of-file comment: "genuine fixes belong here
943: directly, not worked around elsewhere") — this fix belongs in the
944: canonical 014.wsr-pal copy, not file-menu's own local binary, so every
945: build.sh across the tree that re-copies from wsr-pal picks it up
946: automatically. Rebuild wsr-pal's system/prisc+x, then re-run any other
947: widget's own `./button.sh compile` (or equivalent build.sh) to pick up
948: the fixed binary — do not assume the fix "traveled" just because the
949: source file did.
950: 
951: FILES: 014.wsr-pal💸️📌️+2/system/prisc+x.c (exec_custom_op, canonical
952:        fix — shared by every project that copies its prisc+x binary
953:        from here via build.sh)
954: 
955: =======================================================================
956: PITFALL 51: PITFALL 22's keyboard_input.c fix only throttled the EOF
957:             (nread==0) retry path — the hard-error (nread==-1, real
958:             errno) path still returns instantly with ZERO sleep,
959:             reproducing the exact same 100% CPU spin PITFALL 22
960:             already claimed was fixed
961: =======================================================================
962: DATE: 2026-07-29
963: SEVERITY: HIGH (same blast radius as PITFALL 22 — one pegged CPU core
964:            per headless test session — plus it actively misled
965:            debugging by mimicking an "already fixed, known" bug)
966: 
967: SYMPTOM: system/keyboard_input showed ~99% CPU in `ps aux` during a
968: headless test run, identical to PITFALL 22's own symptom, DESPITE that
969: project's copy of keyboard_input.c already containing PITFALL 22's own
970: documented fix (`usleep(20000)` inside the retry loop). Wasted time
971: re-diagnosing a "known, already-fixed" bug before noticing the fix was
972: incomplete, not absent.
973: 
974: ROOT CAUSE (system/keyboard_input.c, read_key()) — the exact code
975: PITFALL 22 left behind:
976: 
977:   while ((nread = read(STDIN_FILENO, &c, 1)) != 1) {
978:       if (nread == -1 && errno != EAGAIN) return -1;
979:       usleep(20000);
980:   }
981: 
982: This throttles the nread==0 (EOF) case correctly — PITFALL 22's own
983: fix works exactly as documented for THAT path. But the hard-error
984: branch (`nread == -1 && errno != EAGAIN`) executes `return -1` on the
985: SAME line, before ever reaching the usleep() below it. In this specific
986: sandboxed test environment, read(STDIN_FILENO, ...) on a backgrounded
987: process's stdin returned a real error (not EAGAIN, not a clean 0/EOF)
988: on every single call — so read_key() returned -1 immediately, and
989: main()'s own `if (key == -1) continue;` looped straight back into
990: read_key() with no sleep anywhere in the cycle. Same 100% CPU spin,
991: different trigger condition than the one PITFALL 22 root-caused.
992: 
993: FIX: move the usleep() ahead of the error check so EVERY non-1 read
994: result gets throttled before the function can return, regardless of
995: which branch put it there:
996: 
997:   while ((nread = read(STDIN_FILENO, &c, 1)) != 1) {
998:       usleep(20000);
999:       if (nread == -1 && errno != EAGAIN) return -1;
1000:   }
1001: 
1002: Preserves the existing contract (still returns -1 upward on a real
1003: hard error, main() still treats that as a harmless no-op tick) while
1004: capping the retry rate on EVERY path that can loop, not just the one
1005: PITFALL 22 happened to hit first.
1006: 
1007: VERIFIED LIVE: system/keyboard_input CPU usage dropped to ~0.0% in the
1008: same headless test session shape (button.sh run-app, backgrounded, no
1009: controlling tty) that showed ~99% before this follow-up fix.
1010: 
1011: STANDING RULE: when a "known, already-fixed" pitfall's symptom
1012: resurfaces, read the CURRENT code before assuming the fix regressed or
1013: was never applied — check whether the ORIGINAL fix actually covers
1014: every branch that can reach the buggy loop, not just the one branch it
1015: was written against. PITFALL 22's own fix was correct for the
1016: condition it was diagnosed from (nread==0 on a plain /dev/null-style
1017: stdin) but incomplete for a different failure mode of the same
1018: underlying "no real tty" environment (a hard read() error instead of a
1019: clean EOF) — same root symptom, same file, same loop, genuinely
1020: different code path through it.
1021: 
1022: FILES: 014.wsr-pal💸️📌️+2/system/keyboard_input.c (read_key() — same
1023:        "shared, independently-copied" scope note as PITFALL 22 itself;
1024:        re-check the other copies listed there if this resurfaces
1025:        elsewhere)
1026:

1033: =======================================================================
1034: DATE: 2026-07-29

1042: them (editor), copying the binaries and the button.sh launch
1043: conditional from file-menu's own working pattern. Every file-level
1044: check passed: chtpm_rgb_render produced rgb_frame.raw at the correct
1045: size (640x768x4 = 1,966,080 bytes), and gl_mirror's own
1046: gl_display.receipt.txt checksum matched rgb_frame.receipt.txt's

1048: for "GL actually consumed the right frame." By that evidence alone this
1049: looked done. It was not: the actual content in the frame's non-glyph
1050: regions being non-zero doesn't mean the TEXT itself renders visibly.
1051: 
1052: ROOT CAUSE: chtpm_rgb_render loads ASCII font glyphs from
1053: pieces/registry/fonts/ascii/<code>/glyph.txt, resolved relative to
1054: PRISC_PROJECT_ROOT (the SESSION dir, symlinked back to the project
1055: root) — NOT from a shared/central location, NOT from 014.wsr-pal at
1056: runtime. This is a project-LOCAL requirement, by design (see
1057: fm-widget-fix.md's own "Lesson: Every project/widget that uses
1058: chtpm_rgb_render must have pieces/registry/fonts/ascii/<code>/
1059: glyph.txt in its own directory tree. Glyphs are project-local, not
1060: shared from wsr."). editor never had a pieces/registry/ directory at
1061: all, and its button.sh never symlinked one into the session dir. Every
1062: character in current_frame.txt rendered as invisible pixels — a
1063: checksummed, correctly-sized, but visually blank/black frame.

68: 1. WHAT HAPPENED THIS SESSION (chronological)
69: ================================================================================
70: - Investigated muchi-pal-llm (an LLM chat pal project) and found it had never
71:   been given real chtpm (the family's shared UI/render engine) - it was still
72:   hand-rolling keystroke buffering (buffer_key.c) instead of using real
73:   <cli_io>. Wrote CHAT-INTEGRATION-ARCHITECTURE.txt covering the chtpm
74:   upgrade, a connector abstraction (ui_drive vs direct_ops backends for
75:   hooking into other chtpm-native projects), LLM-to-LLM chat, a new
76:   "/model script" provider kind, and meta-orchestration - all before writing
77:   any code, per this family's own design-doc-first convention.
78: - Corrected a mischaracterization in MPC.txt/the architecture doc: mutaclsym
79:   DOES have real chtpm (it just never needed <cli_io> - a pure digit-dispatch
80:   game). The actual gap was that muchi-pal-llm's own author never checked
81:   whether chtpm_parser_pal itself already supported free-text input.
82: - Moved 4 projects that lived outside yz.muchiverse/2.muchi-verse-0.0/ into it
83:   (mutaclsym, zoo_0000, muchi-pals-refx-13.00, muchi-pal-llm), fixing stale
84:   path references and a couple of real bugs surfaced by the move (a stray
85:   leftover process still writing to the OLD path after a directory rename -
86:   PRISC_PROJECT_ROOT is cached at process startup, not re-read from a live
87:   kernel cwd).
88: - Renamed muchi-pal-llm -> muchi-pal-agent (direct user correction: "it does
89:   much more than llm chat"). Upgraded it onto real chtpm (session isolation,
90:   real <cli_io>, deleted buffer_key.c/main_loop.pal). Added a genuinely new,
91:   zero-API "script" provider_kind (picks 5-10 random words from a plain
92:   wordbank file, no network, no LLM at all).
93: - Found and root-caused a real live bug: typing one message and pressing
94:   Enter once produced FIVE fragmented sends. Root cause: shared-ops/
95:   prisc+x.c's OP_READ_HISTORY opcode's literal-path parsing form never
96:   actually set the position/cursor register - it silently defaulted to
97:   register 0, which this VM hardwires to 0 every single instruction, so the
98:   read position could never advance; it re-read the same stale value

231:   My best reconstruction, and I think this resolves the apparent
232:   contradiction rather than actually being one: gl_mirror.c and palnet_
233:   peer.c are almost certainly two DIFFERENT jobs, not competing
234:   implementations of the same job.
235:     - gl_mirror.c's own job (best guess): mirroring WINDOW GEOMETRY
236:       (position/size on screen) between two LOCAL processes/windows on
237:       the SAME machine, so they can visually coordinate (e.g. so a drag
238:       gesture crossing from one window into another can be geometrically
239:       detected at all). For that, a socket is real overhead for zero
240:       benefit - two local processes can already share a plain file just
241:       as fast, with none of the connection-management complexity a
242:       socket needs. Reverting THIS to a plain geometry file is consistent
243:       with this whole family's standing preference for plain files over
244:       sockets wherever real cross-machine networking isn't actually
245:       needed (chtpm's entire rendering pipeline, gui_state.txt, etc. all
246:       make the same choice for the same reason).
247:     - palnet_peer.c's own job: the actual DATA transfer of a pet's real
248:       state, genuinely peer-to-peer, potentially ACROSS machines - this
249:       is a fundamentally different requirement (real networking) that a
250:       local geometry file cannot serve at all. Nothing in task #6 removed
251:       or touched palnet_peer.c - it's still the real P2P mechanism, still
252:       built, still proven (pal-chat-irc/pal-chain both use it live), and
253:       is exactly what tasks #7/#8/#9 above are meant to wire the drag
254:       gesture INTO.

256:   if my reconstruction is right, they were dropped from the LOCAL window-
257:   geometry-mirroring plumbing specifically, because that part never
258:   needed real networking in the first place, while the actual pet-export/
259:   import step still goes through palnet_peer.c exactly as before. Confirm

297:   [RESPONSE]: This session's entire live-testing method (used for every
298:   feature - the read_history fix, the trigger routing, the irc-agent-0000
299:   bridge) is two matched halves, and it's worth being explicit that BOTH
300:   only ever verify the DATA/LOGIC layer, not real terminal rendering - an
301:   honest limit of this technique, not a small detail:
302:     - KEY INJECTION (the input half): a real, human keypress normally
303:       goes keyboard_input.c (raw terminal mode) -> pieces/keyboard/
304:       history.txt (or, once chtpm's own <cli_io> is involved, gui_state.
305:       txt's own per-keystroke field sync + pieces/apps/player_app/
306:       interact_relay.txt for the Enter/submit keycode specifically -
307:       that's the file main_loop's own read_history call actually polls).
308:       Testing WITHOUT a real terminal/keyboard means writing directly
309:       into those same files by hand - `echo "message_input=hi there" >
310:       gui_state.txt` then `echo "13" >> interact_relay.txt` is a real,
311:       exact stand-in for "a human typed 'hi there' and pressed Enter,"
312:       because it's literally the same file state a real keypress would
313:       have produced - not a simulation of the input, the actual same
314:       data the real input path writes.
315:     - FRAME READING (the output half): rather than looking at a rendered
316:       terminal screen, read pieces/display/current_frame.txt (or a
317:       project's own view.txt before chtpm's compose_frame step) directly
318:       as text - since a real op wrote that file the same way it would for
319:       a real user watching, reading it as plain text is a real check, not
320:       a workaround.
321:   WHY THIS PROVES LESS THAN IT LOOKS LIKE: both halves verify that the
322:   correct DATA landed in the correct FILE - they say nothing about whether
323:   a REAL terminal, running real ANSI escape codes, actually displays that
324:   data correctly to a human. This matters specifically for two properties
325:   neither key-injection nor frame-reading can ever confirm on their own:
326:     - FRAME HISTORY: compose_frame ops generally render a bounded TAIL of
327:       recent turns/events into view.txt (not the entire history every
328:       time, for the same reason no one wants an infinitely growing
329:       render) - reading current_frame.txt confirms the tail's CONTENT is
330:       correct, but not that a real terminal, redrawing that same bounded
331:       region every tick, gives a human the IMPRESSION of continuous
332:       history the way a real chat scrollback would.
333:     - FRAME SCROLL: most of these TUI-style renders clear/redraw the
334:       screen in place each frame (typical ANSI behavior for a live-
335:       updating display) - which usually means the terminal's OWN native
336:       scrollback buffer does NOT preserve old frames the way it would for
337:       a normal append-only command-line log. Whether a user can actually
338:       scroll UP to see older conversation turns depends entirely on
339:       whether compose_frame's own tail window is generous enough, and/or
340:       whether the render deliberately avoids a hard clear-and-redraw in a
341:       way that would let real terminal scrollback accumulate - this is a
342:       genuine, UNTESTED-so-far question, not something file-injection
343:       testing this session ever actually checked.
344:   TAKEAWAY for task #9 (build AND live-test the full drag flow) and any
345:   future testing generally: file-injection is the right FIRST check (fast,
346:   cheap, exactly reproduces real input/output data) - but it is not a
347:   substitute for actually opening a real terminal, running button.sh `run`
348:   for real, and confirming with real eyes that history/scrollback looks
349:   right. Do both, in that order, don't stop at the first one and call a
350:   feature "verified."
351:

354:   and you could also use a script to do your own test frame history dump,
355:   but it sounds like you weren't using something like that? maybe suggest
356:   it in the testing section]

391: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
392: 4.1 Scaffolding Gemma to act like llama-groq-tool-use, but cheaper
393: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
394: THE PROBLEM: groq-tool-use-mac (llama3-groq-tool-use:8b via ollama) is the
395: one proven, reliable tool-calling model in model_list.txt - native tools[]
396: schema, real function-call JSON, build_ollama_request already handles it
397: correctly. It is also SLOW (an 8B model). gemma-lan (gemma3:270m) is ~30x
398: smaller and dramatically faster/cheaper to run on the same LAN Mac GPU, but
399: it has NO native tool-calling schema at all - it just emits plain text.
400: 
401: THE PLAN (concretely, this is what a future agent should be told to build -
402: paste this whole subsection as the prompt if that's easier than summarizing
403: it further):
404: 
405:   "Gemma doesn't support native tool-calling, so instead of tools[] JSON, we
406:   are going to teach it a plain-text KEYWORD CONVENTION and parse that
407:   convention out of its raw completion text - the exact same shape
408:   send_message.c already uses for provider_kind=llamacpp, which ALSO has no
409:   native tool schema and is already handled by manually building a raw
410:   prompt (text_to_pal_prompt.c) instead of relying on the API to understand
411:   tools[]. Reuse that same pattern for gemma, don't invent a new one:
412: 
413:   1. Write a new system-prompt template (or extend text_to_pal_prompt.c)
414:      that teaches gemma a fixed marker syntax for calling a tool, e.g.:
415:        TOOL: read_file <path>
416:        TOOL: edit_file <path> <old> <new>
417:        TOOL: exec <command>
418:        TOOL: speak <text>
419:      one call per turn, plain text, no JSON required at all.
420:   2. Add a new parser (same shape as json_parser.c, but scanning for the
421:      `TOOL: <verb> <args>` line instead of parsing JSON) that runs on
422:      gemma's raw completion text and extracts verb+args if present.
423:   3. Dispatch the SAME tool ops that already exist and are already proven
424:      (file_ops.c, edit_file.c, cmd_exec.c, list_dir.c, search_in_files.c,
425:      web_search.c - do not rewrite these, they are provider-agnostic
426:      already) - only the PARSING layer is new, the EXECUTION layer is
427:      reused untouched.
428:   4. TTS is the one genuinely new op needed: write ops/tts_speak.c, self-
429:      contained like every other op, shelling out to whatever local TTS
430:      binary is available (espeak/piper/similar;[!.user: we use "edge-tts , .c script"]) with the text argument -
431:      matching the family's own 'op wraps one external program' convention
432:      (see chat_post_message.c or connect_op.c for the shape).
433:   5. Feed the tool's result back into context_log.txt as a tool-result turn
434:      (append_log_turn already supports a 'tool_name' field for exactly this
435:      - see how execute_tool.c does it for the existing PENDING_PERM flow),
436:      then let the SAME check_response.c/send_message.c tick loop already in
437:      main_loop_chtpm.pal re-invoke gemma with the updated context - this is
438:      already 'reloop using context,' no new engine work needed, it's the
439:      exact same mechanism every other provider_kind already uses.
440:   6. Add a new provider_kind, e.g. 'ollama_keyword' (or extend 'ollama' with
441:      a sub-flag), so model_list.txt can point gemma-lan at this new request-
442:      builder instead of the native-tools one groq-tool-use-mac uses.
443: 
444:   Net effect: same read-file/edit-file/exec/speak/prompt-user/reloop
445:   capability as groq-tool-use, at a fraction of the compute, because gemma3:
446:   270m is that much smaller than the 8B tool-use model - the tradeoff is
447:   slightly less reliable tool-call formatting (a plain-text convention is
448:   easier to get wrong than a schema-validated JSON call), which is worth
449:   testing empirically once built, not assumed.; refine "
450: 
451:   [CORRECTION, 2026-07-21, muchi-pal-agent session - this plan was
452:   SUPERSEDED, not built as written above]: steps 1-2 here (teach gemma a
453:   `TOOL: verb args` marker syntax, then parse THAT syntax back out of
454:   gemma's own completion text) were empirically tried and then explicitly
455:   reversed by direct instruction: "gemma should never parse tool etc. we
456:   got tool to work, while using gemma by pre-prompting our own fsm/or bt
457:   and running a script." What actually got built instead - and is the
458:   CURRENT, standing design - is documented in muchi-pal-agent's own
459:   `!.GRAND-PLAN-TOOL-SCAFFOLD.txt`: a three-layer architecture where tool
460:   intent is detected and the tool is EXECUTED BEFORE gemma is ever called
461:   at all (gemma_strategy.c's keyword detection -> strategy_execute_a.c's
462:   pre-execution -> the result gets logged as a normal tool-result turn),
463:   and gemma's own persona (pieces/registry/personas/prompt_keyword.txt)
464:   now explicitly tells it "you never call tools yourself." check_response.
465:   c's own gemma branch no longer scans for ANY marker syntax in gemma's
466:   reply - whatever gemma says is unconditionally plain conversational
467:   text. This achieves this section's own stated goal (gemma gets
468:   tool-use capability despite having no native schema, at a fraction of
469:   groq-tool-use's compute) via a DIFFERENT mechanism than originally
470:   planned - pre-execution instead of a parsed-completion convention - and
471:   the pre-execution version is what's live today. Do not resurrect the
472:   TOOL:-marker-in-gemma's-own-completion approach without a fresh, direct
473:   instruction to do so.
474: 
475:   [CORRECTION, per your note above]: I originally hedged with "espeak/
476:   piper/similar" for the TTS op - wrong, we already have a real answer:
477:   ops/tts_speak.c should shell out specifically to edge-tts (a ".c script"
478:   wrapper around it, matching this family's own "one op wraps one external
479:   program" convention - same shape as chat_post_message.c wrapping nothing
480:   external at all, or connect_op.c wrapping curl). Concretely: tts_speak.c
481:   takes the text to speak as an argument (or reads it off state.txt/a temp
482:   file, matching how other ops take their input), builds an edge-tts
483:   invocation (edge-tts --text "<text>" --write-media <tmp.mp3> or
484:   equivalent, whatever edge-tts's own actual CLI shape is - check its real
485:   usage before hardcoding flags, don't assume), then plays the resulting
486:   audio via whatever local player is already available (aplay/ffplay/
487:   similar - same "shell out to one already-installed program" pattern).
488:   Don't build a generic "any TTS engine" abstraction unless a second engine
489:   is actually needed later - one concrete, working edge-tts op first.
490: 
491: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
492: 4.2 Getting IQABELLA (the iqabod curriculum model) past "random words"
493: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
494: CURRENT STATE: provider_kind=iqabod curricula are narrow, purpose-built test
495: corpora (per ROADMAP-models.txt sec. 4), producing mostly <UNK> output
496: against real conversation - not because the mechanism is broken, but because
497: nobody has trained it on anything resembling real chat yet.
498: 
499: THE PLAN (in order, each step depends on the last actually working):
500:   1. LIVE CHAT DISTILLATION (CHAT-INTEGRATION-ARCHITECTURE.txt sec. 4,
501:      designed, not yet built - but irc-agent-0000's own machinery from THIS
502:      session is 90% of what's needed): put a strong TEACHER (gemini [!.user :prefer gemma!] , since
503:      the Gemini API is "semi-reliable" and available - see budget notes
504:      below) and IQABELLA (student, provider_kind=iqabod) in the SAME real
505:      pal-chat-irc room, each as its own independent muchi-pal-agent
506:      instance/session (session isolation already makes this safe - two
507:      instances never share ephemeral state). Concretely: irc_agent_poll.c's
508:      own polling pattern can be adapted almost directly - the teacher
509:      instance auto-responds to the student's messages instead of picking
510:      random words, using its real gemini connection.
511:   2. HARVEST: build the one new op the architecture doc named but never
512:      built - ops/harvest_room_to_corpus.c <room> <author_filter>
513:      <output_name> - tails rooms/<room>/messages.txt, filters to IQABELLA's
514:      own authored lines only, writes them as a plain corpus file (same
515:      shape generate_corpus.+x already produces).
516:   3. TRAIN: feed that harvested corpus into the EXISTING vocab_only + train
517:      pipeline (ROADMAP sec. 11.1/12.2) - this part is not new, just needs a
518:      real corpus to point at instead of the placeholder test ones.
519:   4. ITERATE: run more chat sessions with the newly-retrained IQABELLA,
520:      harvest again, retrain again - each cycle should measurably reduce
521:      <UNK> output and increase coherent word choice, before ever attempting
522:      harder tasks.
523:   5. RL LAYER, once basic coherence exists: rather than a second expensive
524:      training run every time, use human-in-the-loop feedback (thumbs up/
525:      down on individual IQABELLA turns, logged alongside context_log.txt)
526:      to bias future word/response choices - the same 'cheap, continuous,
527:      personal tuning instead of rare/expensive retraining' philosophy this
528:      whole family's local-AI approach is built on (see the AI-comparison
529:      explainer given earlier this session, TPMOS_TRIPLE_REPORT.md sec. 2).
530:   6. TOWARD CODING: once general chat coherence is real, repeat the exact
531:      same teacher/student/harvest/train loop with a CODE-focused teacher
532:      curriculum instead of general chat - gemini generating small, focused
533:      code-completion pairs (e.g. 'write a function that...' / the
534:      completion) as the room's own conversation content, harvested and
535:      trained the same way. Start with the smallest reasonable unit (single
536:      function completions), only scale up to multi-file/agentic coding
537:      tasks once that smallest unit is reliably working - same 'prove one
538:      thing end-to-end before generalizing' discipline this whole session
539:      followed for every other feature.
540: 
541:   [CORRECTION, per your note above]: I defaulted the teacher role to
542:   gemini - wrong direction given the whole point of this section (4.3) is
543:   reducing paid/rate-limited API reliance. PREFER GEMMA as the teacher:
544:   gemma-lan is local, fast, and effectively free to call as many times as
545:   a distillation loop needs (which could be hundreds/thousands of turns),
546:   where gemini has real cost/rate-limit pressure at that volume. The
547:   teacher/student roles in step 1 become gemma-lan (teacher) + IQABELLA
548:   (student, provider_kind=iqabod) in the same pal-chat-irc room. Only
549:   escalate the TEACHER role itself to gemini if gemma's own output proves
550:   too weak/incoherent to actually teach anything useful - i.e. apply the
551:   exact same escalation tiering from 4.3 to the teacher choice itself, not
552:   just to ordinary chat use. Worth noting honestly: if gemma needs the
553:   keyword-based tool-use scaffolding from 4.1 to be a GOOD teacher (e.g.
554:   to demonstrate real file/tool use, not just conversation), build 4.1
555:   before leaning on gemma-as-teacher for anything beyond plain chat
556:   distillation.
557: 
558: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
559: 4.3 API budget strategy - use Gemini/Haiku deliberately, not by default
560: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
561: Direct user framing: Gemini API access is "semi-reliable" and available;
562: Haiku (or another cloud model) is available "when we have to," but the goal
563: is to reduce reliance on both and budget deliberately, not reflexively call
564: the biggest/most expensive model for everything.
565: 
566: Concrete tiering to actually implement (this is a policy for a future agent
567: to apply when choosing which /model to invoke for a given task, not new
568: code):
569:   1. DEFAULT to the cheapest capable option for the task at hand - script/
570:      wordbank or a small local ollama model (gemma-lan) for anything routine
571:      or repetitive.
572:   2. ESCALATE to gemini-flash only when the task genuinely needs broader
573:      reasoning/knowledge the small local model can't reliably produce -
574:      this is the 'teacher' role in the IQABELLA distillation loop (4.2) and
575:      the 'occasional hard question' role in ordinary use (see the earlier
576:      'giant model for rare/novel questions, small tuned model for everything
577:      routine' explainer).
578:   3. RESERVE Haiku/larger cloud models for cases gemini itself can't handle
579:      reliably (hence 'semi-reliable') - genuinely last resort, and track
580:      when/why escalation happened so the pattern of 'what actually needs the
581:      expensive model' becomes visible over time and can inform what
582:      IQABELLA/gemma should be trained on NEXT (i.e. the escalation log
583:      itself is a curriculum-design signal).
584:   4. Every distillation/training run (4.2) should measurably shrink the set
585:      of tasks that need escalation at all - the budget strategy and the
586:      training strategy are the same feedback loop, not two separate
587:      concerns.
588: 
589: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
590: 4.4 Modularizing mutaclysm's AI like wsr-pal/muchi-pal-agent
591: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
592: THE ASK: mutaclysm's own AI logic should be restructured to look like this
593: session's own muchi-pal-agent/wsr-pal pattern - modular, reusable ops
594: instead of one hardcoded routine.
595: 
596: WHAT THAT PATTERN ACTUALLY IS, concretely, to replicate:
597:   - A REGISTRY file (model_list.txt's own shape: pipe-delimited rows,
598:     id|kind|param|param) that names swappable behaviors instead of hard-
599:     coding one path.
600:   - A SWITCH command (switch_model.c's own shape: look up an id in the
601:     registry, rewrite a handful of state.txt fields, done) so behavior can
602:     change without a rebuild.
603:   - Small, single-purpose, SELF-CONTAINED ops (own root resolution, own
604:     constants, no shared headers - matches every op in this family) instead
605:     of one large function handling every case inline.
606:   - A TICK-DRIVEN poll pattern (check_response.c/irc_agent_poll.c's own
607:     shape: cheap no-op check first, real work only when actually active)
608:     for anything that needs to run continuously rather than once per
609:     keypress.
610:   - A TRIGGER/ROUTING layer (this session's own trigger_list.txt addition
611:     to provider_kind=script) wherever "different input should pick a
612:     different behavor" - directly reusable for "different game situations
613:     should pick a different mutaclysm AI behavior."
614:   FIRST CONCRETE STEP for a future agent: read mutaclysm's current AI code
615:   in full (not assumed), identify the single hardcoded decision points, and
616:   replace each with a registry+switch pair the same shape as switch_model.c
617:   - one at a time, verified working after each swap, not a big-bang rewrite.
618:   
619:   [!user each npc should have its own "piece dir structure" or at least a "npc bank, aka {SCALE_BANK} micro structure which stores the same information but can manage multiple npc types ; this is also something
620:   we will reuse in wsr , when we start adding humans(employees customers)/organisms(animals/food/plants/microbial biology for tech, etc)]
621: 
622:   [RESPONSE]: Good addition, and it's actually a natural extension of the
623:   registry pattern already described above, not a new mechanism - the
624:   distinction worth being precise about is ONE FILE, MANY ROWS (like
625:   model_list.txt/trigger_list.txt already are - one npc_bank.txt with one
626:   pipe-delimited row per NPC type) vs. ONE DIRECTORY PER NPC (like pieces/
627:   world_01/session_01/chat/ is its own real directory with its own state.
628:   txt/context_log.txt). Concretely: use the BANK (single file, many rows)
629:   for NPC TYPES/TEMPLATES - what kinds of NPC exist and their default
630:   stats/behavior-registry-id - and use a real per-instance PIECE DIR only
631:   for a SPECIFIC, STATEFUL, LIVING NPC instance that needs its own
632:   persistent, mutable state over time (health, inventory, relationship
633:   history) the same way pieces/world_01/session_01/chat/ is one specific,
634:   stateful chat instance. Most NPCs in a bank never need their own
635:   directory at all - only ones that accumulate real individual history do.
636:   This exact bank-vs-instance-dir split is what makes it reusable in wsr
637:   for humans/employees/customers and organisms/animals/plants/microbes
638:   later: the BANK format doesn't care what domain it's naming (npc types,
639:   employee roles, animal species - same pipe-delimited shape), only the
640:   decision of "does this specific instance need its own stateful dir"
641:   changes per domain.
642: 
643: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
644: 4.5 Same pattern for terrain generation, leveling/evolution, and scale
645: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
646: THE ASK: apply the identical modular-registry-and-picker approach (4.4) to
647: terrain generation, play leveling/evolution, and abstracting scale across
648: levels/worlds/corpuses.
649: 
650: HOW THE SAME PATTERN MAPS ONTO EACH:
651:   - TERRAIN GENERATION: a terrain_registry.txt (same pipe-delimited shape)
652:     naming swappable generator ops (e.g. a perlin-noise op, a biome-rules
653:     op, a hand-authored room-layout op) - picking a generator becomes a
654:     registry lookup, not a hardcoded function call, exactly like /model
655:     picks a provider_kind.
656:   - LEVELING/EVOLUTION: this is squarely what ROADMAP-models.txt's own
657:     rl/fsm/script registry (sec. 5.1/6.3's shared bank/picker mechanism,
658:     already designed, partially built via provider_kind=script this
659:     session) is FOR - a "leveling rule" or "evolution step" is just another
660:     named, swappable script/fsm/rl entry in the same picker, not a special
661:     case needing its own new mechanism.
662:   - SCALE ABSTRACTION (a single room vs. a full world vs. many worlds):
663:     the SAME small generator ops should be parameterized to operate at
664:     different granularities rather than needing separate "room generator"
665:     vs. "world generator" code - matches this whole family's own "ops are
666:     generic, state/config decides scope" convention (e.g. irc_agent_poll.c
667:     is one op that works for any room name, not one op per room). A future
668:     agent building this should design the generator ops' own parameters
669:     (size, seed, density, whatever the domain needs) FIRST, prove one
670:     generator works at both a small and a large scale using only parameter
671:     changes, before adding any second generator - the same "vertical slice
672:     first" discipline CHAT-INTEGRATION-ARCHITECTURE.txt sec. 7 and this
673:     whole session followed throughout.
674:   - CORPUSES AT SCALE: the exact same registry+picker idea applies to
675:     training corpora themselves (which curriculum/wordbank is active is
676:     already a registry lookup via model_list.txt) - scaling from a small
677:     starter wordbank (sample.txt, 20 words) to a large real corpus is a
678:     matter of pointing the SAME mechanism at a bigger file, not building
679:     new machinery.
680:

682:     also think more deeply about scale bank , like we will have a config file for games
683:     levels, ai corposes , anything that defines if we want that particular module 2 have large scale
684:     or small scale, for example in a driving game , cars should have large scale, describing, tires, gas , engine mechanics , combustion etc (unless otherwise specified for a simple cheap racing game) but would not necessarily need the humans/animals to have large scale . also its npcs may have large scale information about driving, but not so much about "conversaiont" get it? expound on this in its own section if u want its gonna be a big deal. ]
685: 
686:   [RESPONSE]: Yes - this is a real correction to how I framed 4.5 above.
687:   I described scale mostly as "small world vs. big world" (one dimension).
688:   What you're describing is sharper and more valuable: scale is PER-
689:   ENTITY, PER-DOMAIN, not one global dial. The same NPC can need large-
690:   scale fidelity in one domain (driving mechanics) and small-scale/cheap
691:   fidelity in another (conversation) at the same time. That's genuinely
692:   its own concept, expanded in full in the new section 4.6 right below.
693: 
694: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
695: 4.6 SCALE_BANK - a fidelity-configuration layer, orthogonal to WHAT a
696:     module is (this is the big one - its own section as asked)
697: --------------------------------------------------------------------------
698: THE CORE IDEA: scale is not a property of a GAME or a WORLD as a whole -
699: it's a property of one (ENTITY TYPE, DOMAIN) PAIR at a time. A car in a
700: driving game might need deep, "large scale" simulation of its physics
701: domain (tires, gas, engine mechanics, combustion) while needing almost
702: nothing for its "conversation" domain (cars don't talk). An NPC in that
703: same game might need large-scale fidelity for ITS driving-knowledge domain
704: (so it drives convincingly) but only small-scale/cheap fidelity for its
705: conversation domain (a few canned lines is enough). Two different entities,
706: same domain (driving), can independently need different scale too - a
707: "simple cheap racing game" config might deliberately choose small-scale
708: physics for cars even though large-scale is available, because the
709: PRODUCT doesn't need it this time.
710: 
711: WHY THIS IS A BIG DEAL, concretely: without this, a project either (a)
712: hardcodes one fixed fidelity level for everything (wasteful when cheap
713: would do, or worse, hardcodes CHEAP and can't later add depth without a
714: rewrite), or (b) builds bespoke one-off fidelity switches per feature,
715: each its own special case, none of them reusable. SCALE_BANK makes "how
716: much detail does this need" a first-class, swappable, reusable config
717: decision - the SAME mechanism as every registry described above (a
718: pipe-delimited bank naming options, a switch/config picking one), just
719: keyed by (entity_type, domain) instead of a single id.
720: 
721: CONCRETE SHAPE (same pipe-delimited registry convention as model_list.txt/
722: trigger_list.txt/the npc_bank idea in 4.4 above - not a new file format,
723: a new KEY shape):
724:   entity_type|domain|scale_level|module_id
725:   car|physics|large|tire_engine_combustion_full_sim
726:   car|physics|small|simple_speed_accel_abstraction
727:   car|conversation|small|none
728:   npc|driving_knowledge|large|full_driving_rules_module
729:   npc|conversation|small|random_words_or_light_fsm
730:   npc|conversation|large|full_llm_persona_module
731: 
732:   - entity_type/domain: which kind of thing, and which subsystem/behavior
733:     of it (physics, conversation, biology, driving_knowledge, whatever the
734:     domain needs - not a fixed enum, project-defined).
735:   - scale_level: which fidelity tier this row represents (large/small, or
736:     more tiers later if actually needed - don't over-design past 2 tiers
737:     until a 3rd is genuinely wanted).
738:   - module_id: which actual generator/behavior module (an op, a script/
739:     fsm/rl entry from the existing picker, a wordbank) implements THIS
740:     specific (entity_type, domain, scale_level) combination - reuses
741:     whatever registry mechanism that domain already has (e.g. conversation
742:     scale rows point at provider_kind=script/iqabod/gemma entries the same
743:     way model_list.txt already names them).
744: 
745: A SEPARATE, PER-PROJECT/PER-GAME CONFIG FILE then picks which scale_level
746: is ACTIVE for each (entity_type, domain) pair for THIS specific game/
747: simulation - e.g. "simple cheap racing game" config picks car|physics|
748: small, while "hardcore sim racer" config picks car|physics|large, using
749: the exact SAME underlying SCALE_BANK rows either way. This is the same
750: registry-vs-active-selection split as model_list.txt (the bank) vs.
751: state.txt's current_model_id (the active pick) - not new machinery,
752: the same pattern with a 2-part key.
753: 
754: REUSE ACROSS DOMAINS, directly answering "get it?": this is NOT game-
755: specific. The exact same SCALE_BANK mechanism applies to:
756:   - AI TRAINING CORPORA (4.2/4.5's own "corpuses at scale" point) - a
757:     corpus itself can have a scale_level (a tiny starter wordbank vs. a
758:     full trained curriculum for the same domain).
759:   - WSR's future humans/employees/customers and organisms/animals/plants/
760:     microbes (4.4's own npc_bank note) - an "employee" entity might need
761:     large-scale job-skill modeling but small-scale biology modeling; an
762:     "animal" might need the exact opposite (large-scale biology, no job
763:     skills at all). Same bank shape, same per-(entity,domain) key, totally
764:     different actual content per project.
765: 
766: BUILD ORDER (same "prove one thing before generalizing" discipline as
767: everywhere else in this file): pick ONE real entity type with a genuine
768: two-tier need (e.g. mutaclysm's own car-or-NPC, once 4.4's modularization
769: work has already happened there), write its SCALE_BANK rows, wire ONE
770: config file to pick between them, verify both tiers actually work end to
771: end, BEFORE generalizing SCALE_BANK into a shared, cross-project
772: mechanism. This is genuinely valuable but also genuinely new machinery
773: (unlike 4.1-4.5, which mostly reuse existing patterns) - budget real
774: design-doc-first time for it, matching this family's own standing
775: convention, before writing code.
776: 
777: ================================================================================
778: 5. IF YOU ONLY REMEMBER ONE THING FROM SECTION 4
779: ================================================================================
780: Every single sub-ask in section 4 - gemma tool-use, IQABELLA training,
781: budget tiering, mutaclysm's AI, terrain/leveling/scale - reduces to the SAME
782: three ingredients this whole family already uses everywhere else: a plain-
783: text REGISTRY file naming swappable options, a small SWITCH op that rewrites
784: a few state fields to activate one, and small SELF-CONTAINED ops that do the
785: real work. Nothing in section 4 needs new engine capability - it needs the
786: existing pattern applied one more time, in one more place, verified working
787: after each step before moving to the next.
788:

112:     pdf.ln(10)

118: 
119:     # 2. TOC PAGE

123:

126:     pdf.cell(0, 10, clean_emojis("📋 Table of Contents (目录)"), align="L", new_x=XPos.LMARGIN, new_y=YPos.NEXT)
127:     pdf.ln(5)
128:     pdf.set_font("Main", "", 11)
129: 
130:     index_lines = index_content.split("\n")

134:             continue
135:         if "subgraph" in line or "style" in line: continue
136: 
137:         match = re.search(r"\[(.*?)\]", line)
138:         if match:
139:             title = clean_emojis(match.group(1))
140:             pdf.set_x(pdf.l_margin + 5)
141:             pdf.multi_cell(0, 7, f"• {title}", new_x=XPos.LMARGIN, new_y=YPos.NEXT)
142:         elif line.startswith("### "):
143:             pdf.ln(2)
144:             pdf.set_font("Main", "B", 12)
145:             pdf.cell(0, 10, clean_emojis(line[4:]), new_x=XPos.LMARGIN, new_y=YPos.NEXT)
146:             pdf.set_font("Main", "", 11)
147:

149:

155:

167:                     code_buffer = []

170:                     in_code_block = True
171:                 continue
172: 
173:             if in_code_block:
174:                 code_buffer.append(line)
175:                 continue
176: 
177:             line = line.strip()

181:

891: 12. CHTPM-LEVEL MENU NAV MUST BE ${piece_methods}-DRIVEN, NOT A
892:     HARDCODED BUTTON LIST - the SAME rule as section 10's own PAL-VS-C
893:     doc, applied to layouts, not ops
894: ========================================================================
895: 
896: A chtpm layout with only ONE navigable `<button>` (e.g. a bare
897: "Control Hero") makes arrow-key/digit menu nav LOOK broken even though
898: the underlying mechanism (`chtpm_parser_pal.c`'s own real, unmodified
899: `focus_index` nav) is completely correct - there's simply nothing to
900: move between. Real `fuzz-op_manager.c` (confirmed via a full, not
901: excerpted, read) has ZERO menu-cursor logic of its own at all - the
902: "[>]"-navigable menu a real player sees is ENTIRELY `chtpm_parser.c`'s
903: own generic top-level nav, applied to `${piece_methods}` - a real,
904: already-present, unmodified mechanism (`load_dynamic_methods()` in
905: `shared-ops/chtpm_parser_pal.c`) that reads the CURRENT `active_target_id`
906: piece's own `piece.pdl` METHOD table and generates real
907: `<button onClick="KEY:n">` rows automatically. `onClick="KEY:n"`
908: dispatch is already fully wired (`send_command()` -> `inject_raw_key()`
909: -> writes straight to whatever `<interact src>` names) - a layout
910: just has to USE `${piece_methods}`, no other code changes needed.
911: 
912: To wire a new project's own piece up to this (mutaclsym's own real
913: walkthrough, see `!.handoff-july17.txt` sec. 9c for the full account):
914:   1. Confirm the piece already has a real `piece.pdl` with METHOD
915:      rows for its own real actions (most already do, for existing
916:      footer text generation via `pdl_reader.+x list_methods`).
917:   2. Check every non-reserved METHOD key against
918:      `load_dynamic_methods()`'s own real reserved-key list (`move`/
919:      `select`/`interact`/`stat_decay`/`on_turn_end`) - rename any
920:      internal/non-player-facing method (e.g. a literal `end_turn` key)
921:      to match if it isn't already recognized, so it doesn't show up
922:      as a spurious extra numbered button and shift every real option's
923:      digit binding by one.
924:   3. `resolve_dynamic_pdl_path()` (real, unmodified upstream) only
925:      tries wraith-alpha-shaped candidate paths - if a project's own
926:      piece.pdl doesn't live at one of those, add a SYMLINK matching
927:      one of the resolver's own EXISTING fallback paths (its very last
928:      one is `pieces/<id>/<id>.pdl`) rather than patching the fork with
929:      a new candidate path - keeps the "exactly 2 patches" discipline
930:      (sec. 6) intact.
931:   4. Add `active_target_id=<piece_id>` to whatever state file the
932:      project's own `load_vars()` "vars bridge" already reads
933:      unconditionally (`pieces/apps/player_app/state.txt` for a
934:      single-controllable-entity project like mutaclsym's own hero) -
935:      for a project where the controllable entity CHANGES at runtime
936:      (zoo_0000's own xlector/pet switching), this needs to track the
937:      CURRENT selection, not a fixed value - a real, separate piece of
938:      wiring, not yet done anywhere in this family as of 2026-07-17.
939:   5. Use `${piece_methods}` directly in the layout instead of
940:      hardcoding `<button onClick="KEY:n">` rows - matches real
941:      `fuzz-op.chtpm`'s own exact usage.
942: 
943: A static, hardcoded button list (matching each project's own existing
944: digit numbering) is an acceptable SHORT-TERM stopgap while the above
945: isn't wired up yet - but treat it as a stopgap, not the target design.
946: 
947: ========================================================================
948: 13. VARIABLE-LENGTH SUBMENUS (craft/inventory-shaped panels) MUST BE
949:     REAL CHTPM ACTIVATE CONTAINERS - NOT A HAND-DRAWN TEXT OVERLAY WITH
950:     ITS OWN CURSOR
951: ========================================================================
952: 
953: Real, authoritative reference: `DYNAMIC_SUBMENUS_FUNCTIONAL_STD.md`
954: (1.TPMOS, dated 2026-05-19, cited against `projects/groq-ollama/
955: manager/groq-ollama_manager.c`). Core rule: the PARSER owns nav/focus/
956: highlighting entirely; the MANAGER only ever PROJECTS raw button
957: markup - always, never gated behind a mode flag, and never hand-drawing
958: its own "[>]"/"[ ]" markers. mutaclsym's craft/inventory panels
959: originally violated this (compose_frame.c drew a text box with its own
960: `panel_cursor`-based "[>]"/"[ ]" markers OVER the map) - real, live
961: symptom this caused: the outer flat menu's own "1. [Control Hero]" and

963: panel's items were fundamentally unreachable by chtpm's real arrow/
964: digit nav (which only ever knew about the 8 static outer items) -
965: confirmed against a direct request to research fuzz-op/+-demo/
966: wraith-alpha's own real "dynamically scaling" submenu mechanism.
967: 
968: Real fix, mutaclsym's own walkthrough (2026-07-17):
969:   1. Hide the panel's OUTER trigger method from `${piece_methods}`'s
970:      auto-generated flat list via `load_dynamic_methods()`'s existing,
971:      unmodified leading-underscore skip rule (rename the piece.pdl
972:      METHOD key, e.g. `craft` -> `_craft` - update every op/choice.c
973:      strcmp that reads it by name to match).
974:   2. Hand-author the trigger as a real `<button onClick="ACTIVATE">`
975:      directly in the layout (NOT derived from piece.pdl - matches real
976:      groq-ollama.chtpm's own "[3] Switch API" example, which is fully
977:      bespoke markup too), with a `<button label="Close" onClick="BACK"
978:      />` child (chtpm's own native submenu-exit, no relay to the
979:      module at all) followed by a `${some_panel_items}` variable.
980:   3. The manager (this project's own compose_frame.c-equivalent) must
981:      build `${some_panel_items}` as REAL `<button onClick="KEY:n">`
982:      rows (no hand-drawn cursor markers) and write it into
983:      `pieces/apps/player_app/manager/gui_state.txt` EVERY frame,
984:      unconditionally - confirmed real, already-loaded unconditionally
985:      by `load_vars()` for a modern-layout project with no project_id
986:      (mutaclsym/zoo_0000's own shape).

989:      contract is integer-only, confirmed via direct read of
990:      `main_loop_chtpm.pal`): every ACTIVATE submenu's items end up
991:      reusing the SAME single-ASCII-digit relay space as every OTHER
992:      submenu (mutually exclusive since only one can be entered at
993:      once) - this caps each submenu at 9 real entries, and means the
994:      RECEIVING op cannot tell which submenu a bare relayed digit came
995:      from by the digit's value alone. Disambiguate using the real,

1000:      reading this exact file while entering each submenu, never
1001:      assumed from the layout's own document order) as a named
1002:      constant. Re-verify these constants any time the layout's own
1003:      element order changes. A real string-command relay (matching the
1004:      doctrine's own preferred, uncapped `SET_`/`OP:` mechanism) is the
1005:      honest long-term fix, not yet built anywhere in this family.

1010:      MUST apply the identical skip rules `load_dynamic_methods()`
1011:      uses (same reserved keys, same leading-underscore skip) - a
1012:      shared, generic tool like `pdl_reader.+x list_methods` is

1014:      this filtering has to happen in the OP's own consumption of it,

1024:      the module), a value left over from ANY earlier session using the
1025:      old mechanism (or old testing) will PERMANENTLY misroute every
1026:      future digit dispatch through dead code, forever, in a real chtpm
1027:      session - confirmed live. Gate any reliance on that old field on
1028:      which mode is actually running (`pieces/apps/player_app/
1029:      state.txt`'s own `module_path` - contains "main_loop_chtpm" for

1038:      otherwise (an old panel's text box drawn ON TOP OF the map, over

1043: 14. XLECTOR POSSESSION - REAL v1 (piece.pdl-driven) vs. THE FULL

1045: ========================================================================
1046: 
1047: Real, authoritative reference: `projects/fuzz-op/manager/
1048: fuzz-op_manager.c`, lines 505-852 (read in full, not excerpted, per
1049: direct instruction to "look at it very closely"). The REAL mechanism:
1050: 
1051:   - A single global `active_target_id` (default `"xlector"`).
1052:     Movement/dispatch are FULLY GENERIC - `execl(move_entity_trait,
1053:     active_target_id, dir_str, ...)` and `pdl_reader.+x
1054:     <active_target_id> get_method <name>` operate on WHATEVER
1055:     active_target_id currently names. There is no per-entity-type
1056:     special-casing anywhere in the movement/dispatch path itself.
1057:   - Enter, while `active_target_id == "xlector"`: scan every piece
1058:     directory for one sitting at xlector's own current tile
1059:     (excluding "xlector" itself and anything matching a hardcoded
1060:     "zombie" substring - real fuzz-op's own AI-exclusion rule).
1061:     Redirect `active_target_id` to the found piece's directory name -
1062:     this alone IS "possession," nothing else changes.
1063:   - `'9'`/ESC/joystick button 8, checked FIRST, unconditionally: if
1064:     active_target_id != "xlector", snap back to "xlector" immediately.
1065:   - "Shadow xlector sync": after moving a POSSESSED entity, xlector's
1066:     OWN position is silently updated to match it, so un-possessing
1067:     always drops the cursor exactly where you were.
1068:   - xlector is a REAL, standalone piece (own directory/state.txt/
1069:     piece.pdl) in the real reference - NOT two fields bolted onto
1070:     another piece's state file.

1073: hardcoded "zombie" substring exclusion is a WORSE fit for THIS
1074: family's own piece.pdl-driven philosophy (already established this
1075: same session for METHOD tables/§12) - a real, DECLARATIVE
1076: `STATE | possessable | 1` field a piece.pdl author sets is the
1077: better-idiomatic equivalent, checked via a generic
1078: `piece_pdl_state_int()` helper (defaults to possessable=1 when the
1079: field is absent - "opt out, not opt in," matching the spirit of real
1080: fuzz-op only excluding a NAMED thing, not requiring an explicit
1081: allow-list). Mutaclsym has exactly ONE real player-controllable piece

1085: default to controlling outside interact_mode already) - but the CHECK
1086: is now real and piece.pdl-driven, not assumed, and already correctly
1087: refuses to "possess" a monster standing on the same tile as hero.
1088: 
1089: NOT YET BUILT - the full generic mechanism, needed the moment a SECOND
1090: real player-controllable piece exists (a companion/pet, matching real
1091: fuzz-op's own pets):
